Katalog Berita (Halaman 43/57)

#2926. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2924. Packers Injury Report: Green Bay rules out 4 vs. Vikings

The Green Bay Packers have officially ruled out four players on Friday: running back Josh Jacobs (finger), edge defender Micah Parsons (knee/illness-PUP), center Sean Rhyan (ankle) and tight end Josh Whyle. Of those three players, only Jacobs played against the New England Patriots last week, and he did not finish the game. Yesterday, head coach Matt LaFleur stated that Jacobs had yet to have surgery on his fractured ring finger, but that it would be coming in the near future.

The Packers used the doubtful status, which they rarely use, on linebacker Zaire Franklin, who has been out for multiple weeks with a shin fracture. He was a non-participant throughout the week's practices.

The questionable players going into Sunday's action are: running back Chris Brooks (ankle-IR), safety Javon Bullard (ankle/knee), offensive lineman Travis Glover (knee), safety Xavier McKinney (foot), receiver Skyy Moore (ankle-IR), edge defender Lukas Van Ness (back) and guard Kevin Zeitler (ankle/triceps).

In total, 9 of the active roster's 53 players were given game statuses for this week's action against the Minnesota Vikings, leaving just 44 fully healthy players on the roster. Considering that teams need 48 players to suit up for game days, that's a bit problematic.

My guess? The questionable players are more likely than not to play on Sunday, just because the Packers are going to need bodies — and their injured reserve designations are quickly evaporating. Already, Green Bay has used four of their eight allotted return designations this season — with the practice windows now open for Brooks and Moore. And that's with Jacob Monk, Jonathan Ford and Warren Brinson having the potential to return this season — along with some potential moves, like Jacobs going on IR, coming down the line.

The Packers already were able to withstand not having Rhyan, Whyle and Franklin on the roster in recent weeks, and Jacobs will likely be replaced by MarShawn Lloyd — who missed last week's action — on the game-day roster.

As long as the questionable players are ready to go, Green Bay shouldn't need to shake things up too much. If they aren't, though, their reinforcements are thin right now, and we'll probably get a heads-up on who won't be able to play by way of the team's Saturday practice squad elevations.

#2921. Jalen Green’s fit alongside Devin Booker remains a major concern in Phoenix

How much data do you need before you're compelled to make a decision?

In the NBA, the answer to that question is as unique as the front offices tasked with making those decisions. There are countless philosophies surrounding what constitutes meaningful data, how much weight to place on sample sizes, and when statistical trends are reliable enough to warrant action. But here we are, 11 games into the new season, and I know I'm not the only person with two functioning eyes beginning to question whether the Jalen Green experiment is going to work in Phoenix.

I'll admit that I initially viewed Green's acquisition as more of a necessity than an intentional pursuit. The Suns knew they needed to move Kevin Durant for a multitude of reasons, eventually found a trade partner that was acceptable to him (which is an entirely separate situation worth exploring), and needed to make the salaries work. In my mind, Green was simply part of the financial equation required to facilitate the deal.

After all, the Houston Rockets had four seasons of data to evaluate Green. Four years of watching his development, assessing his strengths and weaknesses, and determining whether he was someone they wanted to build around moving forward. Their willingness to include him in the trade suggested that perhaps they'd already reached a conclusion about his long-term value.

As Sam Amick revealed in his latest piece for The Athletic, Green wasn't merely a contractual necessity or a convenient piece of salary matching. He was someone the Phoenix Suns actively coveted.

"(It) was never gonna get done without Jalen and Dillon (Brooks) in the deal," Ishbia told me earlier this year. "Those are the two players we wanted. It wasn't, 'Hey, how do we fill a salary with these numbers?' It was, 'In order to have Kevin Durant on your team, I want Dillon Brooks to help me with my culture and my identity. I want Jalen Green as a 23-year-old up-and-coming leading scorer.'"

Last season was supposed to be the year of evaluation for Green, a player who arrived in Phoenix with an unbelievably clean bill of health, only to appear in 32 games due to lower extremity injuries. So there's data point number one: 32 games. Of course, there's only so much you can reasonably take away from that sample, considering Green was essentially joining a race that was already three-quarters of the way finished by the time he got involved.

Which brings us to this season, the one in which the Suns would finally have an opportunity to evaluate what they actually have in Jalen Green. And the early returns? Less than optimal would probably be the polite way of putting it. Getting benched for the entire fourth quarter against the Charlotte Hornets, a team Green should theoretically thrive against, might be a more accurate description of where things currently stand.

And quite honestly, it was the right decision.

The Suns had been cooking, putting together an impressive 38-16 run in the first half and establishing the kind of offensive rhythm that has been painfully difficult to sustain this season. Much of this occurred with Green on the bench. But when Green checked into the game with 6:06 remaining in the second quarter, that momentum came to a screeching halt.

Charlotte proceeded to unleash a 31-11 run that extended into the third quarter, completely erasing the progress Phoenix had made. During Green's stint on the floor, from 6:06 remaining in the second quarter until he was substituted out with 5:26 left in the third, the Suns were outscored by 18 points. And that was the last we saw of Jalen Green.

Once again, Phoenix struggled to generate productive minutes with Green on the floor, and I believe that gets to the heart of the problem. It's becoming increasingly difficult to identify how his presence complements the players around him, particularly when the team appears to function more effectively without him. And for a player the organization actively coveted as a centerpiece of the Kevin Durant trade, that's a pretty troubling development.

No one is questioning Jalen Green's athleticism. No one is questioning his desire, his attitude, his likability, or his character. By all accounts, he's a stand-up guy who has reached the highest level of professional basketball largely because of his extraordinary physical gifts. His explosiveness, speed, and ability to create separation are undeniable.

But when you reach the NBA, physical gifts can only take you so far. At some point, the difference between being a good player and becoming a great one comes down to your ability to understand the game, recognize situations, make the right decisions, and contribute within the structure of a functioning offense.

When you're the second highest paid player on the roster, earning $36.3 million this season, the Phoenix Suns need you to be great. They need you on the floor in the fourth quarter, positively influencing the outcome of basketball games. They need you making intelligent decisions, creating opportunities for your teammates, and understanding when to attack and when to allow the offense to develop.

What they don't need is someone disrupting the offensive rhythm, launching ill-timed three-point attempts that have little connection to what the team is trying to accomplish, or throwing the basketball directly into the hands of the opposition. Unfortunately, we've seen far too much of the latter. Green, who ranks 53rd in salary this season, is ranked 113th in scoring (11.8 points per game), 92nd in assists (3.1 assists), 189th in three-point percentage (27.0%), and 246th in NET +/- (-28.0).

The Ringer's Rob Mahoney recently said on the Bill Simmons podcast that Green has gone "from counterproductive to actively bad this season." I can't say I disagree.

Perhaps some of these struggles can be attributed to the Suns asking Green to operate in a role he's never truly occupied before, that of a primary facilitator. It's a reasonable explanation, considering his development in Houston was largely centered around his ability to score rather than orchestrate an offense. But as the games have progressed, I'm beginning to wonder whether that's even what Phoenix is asking him to do anymore.

His assist percentage currently sits at 17.1%, placing him in the 44th percentile this season. That doesn't exactly suggest a player being tasked with consistently running the offense. It feels more like the Suns want him to occasionally facilitate, score when opportunities arise, and somehow make everything fit together. Therein lies the problem. Green's presence continues to look awkward within this offense's structure. His movements feel disconnected from those around him, his decisions frequently interrupt the flow of possessions, and his inability to consistently operate within the team's offensive framework has become increasingly detrimental.

That's what makes this entire situation such a quagmire. Green is struggling to operate within the confines of everything happening around him, and much of what we're witnessing mirrors the problems we saw last season. At the time, we could point to his injuries, the late start, and the lack of continuity as reasonable explanations for his struggles. Those were legitimate factors, and they made it difficult to draw any definitive conclusions.

But now? We're beginning to see some familiar patterns emerge. And the Suns, as they seem to be in almost every avenue concerning their ability to build for the future, find themselves stuck between a rock and a hard place.

Green's trade value isn't particularly encouraging at the moment. If Phoenix were to explore moving him, the return would likely be underwhelming. Any opposing team willing to absorb his $36.3 million salary would have to believe there's something worth salvaging, and even with his contract becoming an expiring deal next season, there's a reasonable chance that team would want draft compensation to make the transaction worthwhile.

What exactly are the Suns supposed to offer? They don't have an abundance of desirable draft assets sitting around, waiting to be attached to another expensive contract in hopes of making a problem disappear. They used those up to get off of the Nurkic deal or to acquire Mark Williams and Miles Bridges. We've already watched this organization sacrifice enough of its future trying to correct mistakes from its past.

So what do you do? One possibility is moving Green to the bench, and quite honestly, that might be the most sensible solution available. We've seen a version of this experiment before. In January 2025, Mike Budenholzer finally came to the realization that pairing Devin Booker with another ball dominant shooting guard in Bradley Beal was creating more problems than it was solving. The overlapping skill sets were limiting the team's offensive ceiling, and something needed to change.

We all remember how that went. Beal wasn't particularly receptive to the adjustment, and the situation became another chapter in the ongoing dysfunction surrounding that roster.

Green, to his credit, appears to have a different attitude toward making adjustments. He's expressed a willingness to evolve his game, embrace more facilitating responsibilities, and has spent time working with Steve Nash to improve that aspect of his skill set. Everything suggests he wants to fit in, wants to improve, and wants to become the player Phoenix envisioned when it acquired him. I don't question any of that. But wanting something to work doesn't necessarily mean it's going to work.

When you're playing alongside Devin Booker, the fit becomes especially problematic. Booker possesses an extraordinary ability to control an offense, manipulate defenses, and create scoring opportunities for himself and others. Those are his superpowers. The players surrounding him should be enhancing those abilities, creating space for him to operate, and making his job easier. They shouldn't be obstacles he has to navigate.

Perhaps the answer is allowing Green to find his footing with the second unit, where he can operate with greater freedom, develop some confidence, and establish a rhythm without constantly having to figure out how his game intersects with Booker's. If he begins finding success in that role, maybe the comfort and confidence he develops can eventually carry over into shared minutes with the starters.

Of course, that raises another question. Who starts in his place? Collin Gillespie would seem to be the most obvious candidate, given his familiarity with the offense, his ability to play alongside Booker, and the success Phoenix has experienced with him on the floor. But at this point, you could certainly sell me on Jordan Goodwin as well. Goodwin might not be the traditional facilitator you're looking for, but he brings something this team desperately needs: a player who can positively influence the game on both ends of the floor without requiring the offense to revolve around him. That's a conversation worth exploring another day.

Ultimately, as I sat there watching the fourth quarter against Charlotte, knowing how ineffective Green had been throughout his minutes, I found myself relieved that he wasn't on the floor. And I don't particularly enjoy admitting that. Here was a player the Suns actively pursued as part of the Kevin Durant trade, someone they envisioned becoming a significant part of their future, and I was genuinely pleased that he was sitting on the bench during the most important stretch of the game.

Instead, Phoenix leaned on a group that looked much more familiar. Much of the lineup resembled the units that helped the Suns win so many games last season, with Luke Kennard occupying the role previously filled by Grayson Allen for much of the stretch. And what happened? The Suns played well. The offense flowed. Players understood their responsibilities, the ball moved with purpose, and Phoenix outscored Charlotte 29-20 in a fourth quarter that was void of his presence.

There was a familiarity to it, a sense that the players on the floor understood how to complement one another rather than compete for the same opportunities. And that's what makes this entire situation so frustrating. The Suns have invested significant money, opportunity, and organizational belief in Jalen Green. They want him to succeed. They need him to succeed. But right now, some of their most effective basketball is being played when he's watching from the sideline.

Eleven games into the season might not be enough data to make a definitive judgment about his future. But it's certainly enough to start asking some uncomfortable questions.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2920. Heat Stars Go Off In NBA Cup Win vs. Cavaliers: Key Takeaways From Eastern Conference Matchup

It hasn't been an easy campaign for the Miami Heat this season. After a 0-4 start, they dug themselves into an early hole and have been trying to recover ever since. Going into tonight's game, however, the Heat (5-6) had momentum coming off a two-game win streak, and they managed to build on it with a clutch 133-121 win over the Cleveland Cavaliers (6-6) on Friday.

Longtime franchise center Bam Adebayo led the way for Miami, pouring in 37 points, 14 rebounds, and three assists on 68.4% shooting from the field (2-3 from three). Giannis Antetokounmpo, meanwhile, added 37 points, nine rebounds, two assists, and one block on 75.0% shooting (1-2 from three). At point guard, Davion Mitchell had a stellar performance with 22 points, five rebounds, 17 assists, and one block on 50.0% shooting (3-7 from three).

For the Cavaliers, superstar Donovan Mitchell was making things happen with 25 points, four rebounds, and seven assists on 42.9% shooting from the field (5-12 from three), while veteran sharpshooter James Harden added 20 points, six rebounds, eight assists, three steals, and one block on 38.5% shooting from the field (2-8 from three). Off the bench, guard Tyrese Proctor dropped 22 points, four rebounds, three assists, and one steal on 77.8% shooting from the field (5-12 from three).

As two teams with nearly identical records, you couldn't ask for a more even matchup, and the game itself reflected their matching talent with multiple lead changes and a highly competitive atmosphere. In the end, not even 45 points from Cleveland's backcourt were enough to outplay Miami, who now move to 6-6 on the year. It's just one win in an up-and-down season for Miami, but they showed positive signs tonight that gave us plenty of interesting conclusions to draw. The Cavaliers, meanwhile, are back to the drawing board as some concerning trends continue.

Before the season, there were doubts that Davion Mitchell was the right fit for this group. That's why he was tied to various trade rumors this summer, including for stars like Kyrie Irving. Tonight, though, Mitchell proved that he can be the point guard for this era of Miami Heat basketball. With nearly 39 minutes, he saw more time than any of his teammates, and for good reason. From the opening tip, Mitchell played smart, patient basketball, resulting in good shot selection and timely assists.

In total, Mitchell finished with 22 points, five rebounds, 17 assists, and one block on 50.0% shooting and 42.9% shooting from three. He was a game-high +22. Ultimately, while Giannis and Bam may have gotten the most highlights in this game, Davion was the one making everything happen with his presence on both ends of the floor. Head coach Erik Spoelstra noticed this as well, and it's why he put so much trust in him to hold his own against a star-studded backcourt in Mitchell and Harden.

At this point, it's clear that the Heat already have their long-term point guard, and he's more than made his case for a shiny new extension as he sits on an expiring deal worth $12.4 million.

The Cavaliers have everything on paper: a loaded roster, a proven coach, and plenty of playoff experience. This season, after acquiring veteran swingman Peyton Watson over the summer, they were projected to be among the leading teams in the East. So far, though, Cleveland has lacked consistency, and this game was another example of that trend. James Harden and Donovan Mitchell, who are known for their scoring, struggled with efficiency today, shooting 38.5% and 42.9%, respectively.

For most of the game, Cleveland appeared to be going through the motions. They were settling for tough shots and making tough mistakes on defense that cost them greatly. What's worse is that their new addition, Peyton Watson, hasn't provided that necessary boost offensively. Today, he only had four points, four rebounds, and two assists on 20.0% shooting from the field.

For all their talent, the Cavaliers struggle with consistency, and you never really know what to expect from them. Today was another one of their off days, and we saw how it ended. If the Cavs want to rise up the standings, they need to play more focused basketball so they can string together a streak of victories.

Bam Adebayo has struggled in the Giannis era, particularly when it comes to scoring. More than anything, he has looked uncomfortable offensively, raising concerns that he might not make a good pairing with Giannis. Fortunately for Miami, he looked all-too-familiar against the Cavaliers tonight, dropping a season-high 37 points, 14 rebounds, and three assists on 68.4% shooting from the field and 66.7% shooting from three.

In his usual fashion, Bam was active on both ends of the floor against Cleveland, contesting shots and being that defensive anchor to hold Jarrett Allen to just nine points on 75.0% shooting. The most encouraging sign was his decision-making on the other end, as most of his shot attempts were clean looks within the flow of the offense. He was also active on the boards, grabbing a game-high 14 rebounds to give Miami a 46-36 edge in the game.

When Adebayo is playing aggressively like he did today, the Heat are a tough team to stop. Whether or not it's sustainable is highly debateable, but it's a sign things are moving in a positive direction, especially now that the team has won three straight games.

#2916. Michigan State-Washington kickoff moved back due to flight delays

Michigan State football's kickoff time against Washington has been moved to 5:30 p.m. Saturday, Nov. 14.

A school official confirmed the change late Friday night after travel disruption shut down air travel and delayed the Huskies' departure from Seattle after they had initially boarded their plane to fly to Lansing around 5:15 p.m. ET.

UW finally took off around 10:50 p.m. ET and is expected to arrive at Capital City Airport around 2:30 a.m. ET in Lansing.

The Spartans (3-6, 1-5 Big Ten) and Huskies (5-4, 2-4) originally were scheduled to kickoff at 4:15 p.m., Saturday at Spartan Stadium. The game will remain on Big Ten Network.

#2915. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2914. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2913. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations (PDF)

#2912. Dyson Daniels calls Luka the ‘hardest guy to guard’ in the NBA

Luka Dončić continued his excellent play against the Atlanta Hawks Monday night, when he logged 32 points (11-of-22 shooting), 10 assists, eight rebounds, five steals and two blocks in a 124-114 victory. For much of the game, his primary defender was Dyson Daniels, who led the league in steals in 2024-25 and has earned All-Defensive Team nods the past two seasons.

Daniels and Atlanta implemented a variety of schemes to try and slow the Dončić-led Lakers offense but nothing really worked. Los Angeles scored 124 points and posted a 120.4 offensive rating, despite Dončić's co-star at guard, Austin Reaves, sitting out the second leg of the back-to-back. After the game, Daniels bestowed quite the complimentary label upon Dončić to succinctly summarize the Slovenian's offensive dominance.

"He's probably the hardest guy to guard in the NBA right now," Daniels told reporters. "And then, he's also a willing passer, so."

While Dončić flourished individually, his gravity and wondrous playmaking helped role players like Sandro Mamukelashvili (23 points, 7-of-13 shooting), Walker Kessler (21 points, 8-of-12 shooting), Collin Sexton (18 points, 6-of-11 shooting) and Ziaire Williams (12 points, 5-of-10 shooting) produce efficient scoring nights alongside him in Atlanta, which Daniels acknowledged, too.

Dončić and the 11-2, West-leading Lakers have been a buzzsaw offensively this season. Generating 122.5 points per 100 possessions, they own the NBA's top-ranked offense, per Cleaning the Glass. Dončić himself is averaging a preposterous 38.6 points and 7.6 assists per game while shooting 58.7% inside the arc and a career-high 40.8% from deep. He's taking a league-high 13.4 triples each night and making a league-high 5.5 of them. The Lakers are scoring 131.0 points per 100 possessions when he's on the court.

Dončić may be the engine but his backcourt buddy, Reaves, is crucial to Los Angeles' success and also off to a ripping start. He's averaging 24.7 points and 4.7 assists on a .513/.409/.915 shooting split. If those marks hold, he'd join the prestigious 50-40-90 club, which currently features nine NBA players, five of whom are MVPs and eight of whom are All-NBA honorees.

Dončić and the Lakers return to the court Sunday against the Phoenix Suns, when they'll look to push their winning streak to eight games. Tipoff is scheduled for 7 p.m.

#2904. Carmelo Anthony compares potential OG Anunoby NBA Finals MVP to Andre Iguodala

Carmelo Anthony compares potential OG Anunoby NBA Finals MVP to Andre Iguodala originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Even two days after the New York Knicks' record-breaking comeback to defeat the San Antonio Spurs in Game 4 of the 2026 NBA Finals, people are still buzzing over OG Anunoby's game-winning tip-in layup.

Along with a crucial block on San Antonio's De'Aaron Fox's fastbreak layup with 11 seconds left in the game, Anunoby has given himself a case to win Finals MVP if New York finishes its mission to win its first NBA championship since 1973.

Former Knicks superstar Carmelo Anthony supports the idea of Anunoby winning Finals MVP, but without eliminating the fact that New York's franchise player, Jalen Brunson, has led the team in scoring in three of its four games in the NBA Finals so far.

"OG has been the most steady, consistent guy in these Finals," Anthony said on his "7PM in Brooklyn" show.

"Forget the tip-in. That stop on Fox was amazing. So now you put all of that together… that's me playing devil's advocate. But let's just be honest about this, it's [Brunson]. If OG wins it, it's like Andre Iguodala in Golden State."

During the 2015 NBA Finals, Warriors legend Iguodala won the Finals MVP award over the team's best series performer, Steph Curry, who averaged 26.0 points, 5.2 rebounds, 6.3 assists and 1.8 steals per game and shot 38.5 percent from 3-point range.

Meanwhile, Iguodala only averaged 16.3 points, 5.8 rebounds, four assists and 1.3 steals per game. Despite being outproduced by Curry offensively during the series, Iguodala secured the award mainly for his defense against the Cleveland Cavaliers' LeBron James.

Similarly, Brunson currently is outperforming Anunoby when it comes to stats, averaging 29.5 points, 4.5 rebounds, five assists and two steals per game in the 2026 NBA Finals.

Anunoby has lower averages of 23.8 points, four rebounds, 1.3 assists and one steal per game, but is shooting more efficiently at 58 percent from the field and 55.6 percent from beyond the arc.

The 6-foot-7, 240-pound forward certainly has a case for Finals MVP, especially with his heroic plays in Game 4 against the Spurs, but it likely would require another memorable performance in Game 5 for him to beat out Brunson for the prestigious award.

#2897. Why Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg calls Rookie of the Year possibility a ‘lose-lose'

Why Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg calls Rookie of the Year possibility a 'lose-lose' originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Yaxel Lendeborg has a theory about potentially winning NBA Rookie of the Year: no matter what happens, somebody will have a complaint.

Lendeborg, the 11th overall pick in June's 2026 NBA draft, is averaging 21.7 points, 7.4 rebounds and 3.7 assists through his first 12 NBA games, and he was No. 1 on the NBA's Kia Rookie Ladder this week. Asked how badly he wants the award in an interview with NBA on Prime's Taylor Rooks, the Warriors rookie said he wants it, but he does not think it can be a clean win.

"Man, that right there, I mean, I want it pretty badly, but when you think about it, it's kind of like a lose-lose for me, you know?" Lendeborg told Rooks. "If I don't get it, 'Ah, he's 24, he couldn't get Rookie of the Year.' If I do get it, 'Ah, he won it because he's 24 years old.'"

The age question follows him because it is real. Lendeborg is 24 and arrived after a college career at Michigan, where he won an NCAA championship, a path that is older than the typical rookies. He did not dodge it, but he did not let it set the ceiling either.

"So it's like the award would be nice, but I'm trying to be an All-Star this year," Lendeborg said.

That is a rare goal for a rookie. Only 45 rookies have ever made the NBA All-Star Game, and only 10 have done it since the 1985 game, with Blake Griffin in 2011 the most recent.

The next few weeks might help Lendeborg make the case. Through 11 games, he was the only rookie averaging at least 20 points, seven rebounds and four assists.

Now Golden State needs him more. An MRI confirmed that Steph Curry has an oblique abdominal tear, and the Warriors said he will be re-evaluated in two weeks, with ESPN's Shams Charania reporting the team expects him to miss closer to a month. The Warriors have said Lendeborg already is their primary option in minutes without Curry.

Lendeborg scored 25 points with 10 rebounds in Wednesday's loss to the Houston Rockets after Curry left early. If he wants to settle the age debate, the next month without Curry might be his best chance.

#2892. Packers must corral Vikings return man Myles Price

The Vikings' defense is incredible. Their offense should be more capable with a new quarterback under center. But their special teams also present a very interesting challenge for the Packers.

Under the guidance of new coordinator Cam Achord, the Packers' special teams have been — with some exceptions — generally pretty solid. Pre-injury, Skyy Moore bolstered the Packers' return game, but the Packers' special teams have really shined in coverage. As of this week, the Packers' have allowed the fourth-lowest average per return on punt returns and the 10th lowest figure on special teams. (This, incidentally, aligns with the special teams stuff the Packers have historically cared about the most and done the best, but it's still good to see them doing a good job there.)

But as good as the Packers are in punt and kick coverage, the Vikings are even better at punt and kick returns. Minnesota currently ranks first in punt return average (17.5 yards per return) and second in kickoff return average (29.9 per return). It's true the Vikings have logged a very long punt return (86 yards) and a pretty long kickoff return (68 yards), but even if you removed those long returns, the Vikings' averages would only drop them to the fifth- and third-best spots in the league on punt and kickoff return, respectively.

And it's all thanks to return man Myles Price.

Undrafted out of Indiana in 2025, the 5-foot-9, 183-pound Price was a special teams only player for the Vikings last season. He averaged a respectable 9.9 yards per return on 30 punt returns and 25.9 yards per return on 60 kickoff returns, but capped out at a long of 43 yards on his punt duties and 61 yards on kickoff returns. Certainly not bad numbers, but not eye popping.

That's changed in 2026. The pocket-sized Price leads the NFL in punt return yardage, boasts the longest punt return in the league, and is the only player in the NFL to score on a punt return this season. He's also the only player who's returned either a punt or a kick for the Vikings this year, meaning their impressive league-wide rankings are entirely the work of their micro machine return man.

Everybody knows the Packers will have their hands full with Brian Flores, and Kirk Cousins may be enough to bolster their offense to at least some level of respectability. But if the Packers don't take care of business on special teams, the Vikings could end up tilting the balance in that phase of the game. And in what's expected to be a close game, any little bit could make a difference.

Fortunately, special teams have never been a game-altering problem for the Packers in the past, right? Right?

#2890. Why Draymond doesn't blame De'Aaron Fox's attempted layup for Spurs' Game 4 loss

Why Draymond doesn't blame De'Aaron Fox's attempted layup for Spurs' Game 4 loss originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Spurs point guard De'Aaron Fox has been under scrutiny since seemingly costing San Antonio Game 4 of the 2026 NBA Finals, but Warriors star Draymond Green doesn't necessarily view it that way.

The Spurs held a narrow 1-point lead with about 15 seconds remaining when Fox deflected and then secured the ball as time wound down. Instead of dribbling the ball out to waste more time and potentially get sent to the free-throw line, Fox thought he could outrun New York Knicks forward OG Anunoby and get in an easy layup to extend San Antonio's lead to three points. But Anunoby blocked Fox's shot, giving the Knicks one final possession to pull off their miraculous and historic comeback victory after being down by as many as 29 points.

While many are focused on that single play by Fox, Green believes the blame should be pointed elsewhere for the Spurs' loss, and to him, there's plenty of blame to go around.

"Let's dish out a little blame for the Spurs' collapse," Green said on "The Draymond Green Show." "Let's start with De'Aaron Fox. 6-for-16 from the field, not that important. Mindless turnovers in the clutch, definitely got to be better there. The decision to shoot the layup, that's a bang-bang decision. Not a smart play. Very bang-bang. If he makes the layup, no one's complaining. If he misses the layup, like he did, everyone's like, 'Yo, he cost them the season.'

"I think the turnovers were worse than the attempted layup. Yes, it was bad. I'm not saying it wasn't. But that's not what cost them the game. There were so many things down the stretch. … I'll blame those turnovers more than I'll blame the layup."

Fox had a team-leading four turnovers in the crushing loss, with all four of them coming in the second half (three in the third quarter).

He was a plus-23 in the first half and a minus-22 in the second.

But Green also pointed the finger at San Antonio's best player, Victor Wembanyama.

"Wemby. From the moment Wemby started telling Mitchell Robinson, 'I'm in your head,' the Knicks outscored the Spurs 87-69. Wemby scored 14 more points and was 4-for-17 from the field," Green said. "Two big missed free throws at the end from a great free-throw shooter. Bad 3s, wasn't rolling to the rim anymore. I think Wemby deserves a lot of blame. Not complete blame, but again, you're up 29 points and you're that great, you got to be able to stop the bleeding at some point, and he did not."

Wembanyama finished with a team-high 24 points and 13 rebounds, but he shot just 36 percent from the field and 25 percent from beyond the arc.

Finally, Green believes Spurs coach Mitch Johnson made some questionable decisions down the stretch.

"Mitch Johnson, 20-4 run before you take a timeout with a young team like that, I can't agree with," Green said. "Never adjusted in the second half. Rotations were not great. There were times where they had a big enough lead to give Wemby a rest, and he didn't. He left Wemby in. And if you get him some rest in some of those spots that you could have, maybe down the stretch, he could've done some of those things I was just talking about. But he played and he played. I think you could have given him some rest and he could've been fresher down the stretch. He had nothing in the tank.

"Mitch Johnson has to be better. With his subs, his timeouts, when he takes timeouts, getting great shots out of timeouts. I think he could be a lot better. It's his first NBA Finals; everybody goes through their growing pains. Mike Brown has been here before. There are growing pains, and it comes with the territory."

No team is perfect, but in the NBA Finals, it's about limiting the mistakes. And for the Spurs, there were too many costly mistakes in Game 4 to put them down and face elimination on their home floor on Saturday.

#2888. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2882. OG Anunoby will take home 2026 NBA Finals MVP award, Draymond Green predicts

OG Anunoby will take home 2026 NBA Finals MVP award, Draymond Green predicts originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

With the New York Knicks taking a 3-1 lead over the San Antonio Spurs in the NBA Finals, the Larry O'Brien Trophy appears headed to Manhattan.

But who takes home the Bill Russell Trophy for Finals MVP remains more contentious entering Saturday's Game 5 in San Antonio.

Though Knicks star Jalen Brunson is the odds-on favorite to win the award, many fans and analysts believe OG Anunoby has been the best player in the series thus far — including Draymond Green.

"I see a lot of people saying OG Anunoby is the current favorite for the Finals MVP. And I must say, I agree," Green said on Friday's episode of "The Draymond Green Show." "I think he's been the most consistent. I think, you know, he's made pivotal plays. He had the block and the bucket to close out the game in Game 4. Jalen Brunson was incredible last game, but I think the first three games, he wasn't great, was shooting a lot of shots at a low percentage.

"And OG has just been constant. And it hasn't been like averaging 14 points, 16 points — he's averaging 24 points on the series, 65 percent from the field, 55 [percent] from three, playing the defense he's playing, guarding [Victor Wembenyama] at times in the series. I think OG Anunoby has a decent Game 5, and [the Knicks] win Game 5. I think OG Anunoby is the Finals MVP."

The Anunoby vs. Brunson debate feels a bit reminiscent of the 2015 NBA Finals, when Andre Iguodala was named MVP over Steph Curry. Like Brunson, Curry boasted better stats than Iguodala, but voters recognized and rewarded Iguodala elevating his game on the biggest stage and making a massive defensive impact — the same could be said of Anunoby this series.

Perhaps that could be the reason for Green's opinion on the matter this time around, already having seen a similar storyline play out a decade earlier.

Regardless, we'll find out soon enough whether or not Green's prediction comes to fruition.

#2878. Whitaker sparks turnaround as Hamilton Heights upsets No. 7 Bluffton, wins 3A regional

ARCADIA - On Sept. 4, Hamilton Heights boasted a winless record after dropping its third straight to start the season. Two-plus months later, the unranked Huskies (10-3) haven't lost since and are headed to semistate in Class 3A after upsetting No. 7 Bluffton Friday night.

What happened in that first week of September that drastically altered the season's trajectory? Sophomore quarterback Lucas Whitaker happened.

After playing some in Week 3, Whitaker took over as the team's starting signal caller for the fourth game. Huskies' coach Caleb Small noticed a difference immediately.

"Our best week of practice was Week 4," Small said.

Whitaker, now 15, was only 14 when he made his first start against rival Tipton. He's shown poise well beyond his years since taking the job, on full display Friday as he made plays with both his arms and legs. He finished with three touchdowns in the win, two passing and one rushing.

The youngster deflected the hype to his coaches and teammates after the game.

"My coaches, they're advanced," Whitaker explained. "Our coach came from Ben Davis. He knows what he's doing. He's developed me really well."

Huskies senior tailback AJ Flanagan ran well for the hosts, often churning up difficult yards while dragging defenders with him. He produced two crucial rushing touchdowns in the second half as Bluffton kept matching Hamilton Heights blow for blow until the very end.

The Tigers scored with less than a minute to play to bring the score to 48-47 and opted to go for the win on a 2-point attempt. But poor execution led to the ensuing play breaking down as soon as the ball was snapped, allowing Hamilton Heights to hang on for the win after recovering an onside kick.

"We have a great running back and we have great wide receivers," Whitaker said. "Our o-line is very two dimensional. They can run block and pass block. As you saw, I think that was zero sacks today. If I have that much time to throw the ball, it makes everything way easier."

Bluffton senior running back Cooper Craig was a standout for the visitors, regularly gashing the Huskies for huge gains on the ground. He rushed for a game-high five scores, including touchdown runs of 35, 58 and 68 yards.

Tackling was an issue for the Hamilton Heights throughout, but its potent offense more than picked up the slack.

Bluffton (12-1), meanwhile, ends its season on a sour note after entering the contest undefeated.

For Whitaker and the Huskies though, a semistate date with second-ranked Andrean awaits. Indian Creek and Tri-West round out the other side of the Class 3A semistate bracket.

"We're in the final four for the first time in a long time," Small added. "Third time in program history. #2873. Fantasy Basketball Weekend Streamers: Reed Sheppard ready to thrive in increased minutes

With Marcus Smart set to begin a three-game suspension on Friday, Reed Sheppard should be considered the priority streaming option for this weekend. That won't bump Sheppard into the starting lineup, but he has been excellent this season when the minutes are there.

When Sheppard has played at least 20 minutes this season, he is averaging 13.2 points, 3.6 assists, 1.8 steals and 2.8 triples per game. Houston has more depth this season, which has limited Sheppard's role in his third season, but when he has gotten the opportunity, he hasn't disappointed. This is also a great opportunity for him to prove that he deserves a larger spot in the rotation for the rest of the season. For now, we'll just take baby steps and hopefully enjoy a strong three-game stretch of production.

The Hawks, Bulls, Cavaliers, Mavericks, Rockets, Pacers, Clippers, Grizzlies, Heat, Bucks, Pelicans, Knicks, Thunder, Kings and Spurs all play two games this weekend, so prioritize those teams if you're looking to stream this weekend.

He has been one of the best rookies in the league this season and is certainly looking like the best value pick of this past summer.

Wiggins is finding himself to be a regular on the list, mostly because he's been awesome this season, but also because the Hawks continue to have a favorable schedule. Wiggins set season-highs earlier this week with five triples and six assists in a game, and he'll continue to play a significant role off the bench for Atlanta. He has been an excellent addition for them and is a key piece to this team, giving him value beyond this weekend.

#2868. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2867. He’s here, he’s there, he’s every-f—ing-where, VJ Edgecombe

Ted Lasso became an iconic show in short order.

The Jason Sudeikis-led comedy series about an American football coach who becomes a football (soccer) coach became a cultural phenomenon.

One of the most popular characters is Roy Kent, the grizzled old veteran of A.F.C. Richmond. Kent, portrayed by writer and comedian Brett Goldstein, was once a star of the Premier League, but he's on his last legs when we meet him in the show.

Fans of Kent made up a song at his peak, one they serenaded him with as he gets hurt and leaves the pitch for the final time.

He's here, he's there, he's every-fucking-where, Roy Kent, Roy Kent

Though he's only in his second year — and is the polar opposite of "grizzled" — VJ Edgecombe has embodied that song in his young NBA career. There was no clearer example of that than his performance in the Sixers' 123-112 win over the Toronto Raptors Thursday night.

With LeBron James out of the lineup, Edgecombe stepped into a bigger role and looked comfortable in it.

"I'm blessed to have a group of guys around me that's encouraging," Edgecombe said postgame. "They help me with confidence."

Edgecombe scored 15 points with multiple dazzling finishes at the rim, a couple serious dunks (and then teasing the possibility of doing the dunk contest postgame) and a three. He drove to the rim and made the right reads all night, finding his teammates for eight assists. He continued to crash the glass at a high level for a guard, pulling down eight rebounds. He also continued to defend at a high level, recording three steals and a block.

All of this and the general winning plays he makes on a nightly basis have become what's expected of him.

"I just tell him to go do that VJ stuff," Tyrese Maxey said. "Literally, that's exactly what I tell him. I say, 'Hey, man, go do that VJ stuff that you do,' and he literally goes out there and does VJ stuff. He had 15, eight and eight tonight. Who knows? Next game it may be 20, four and four. He just does VJ stuff. He gets us loose balls. He makes winning plays. He defends. He scores. He does it all."

So much of the noise this offseason surrounding the super team of James, Maxey, Joel Embiid and Jaylen Brown the Sixers have built was about Edgecombe. How would he handle his role changing? Would a drop in usage affect his development? Would the glut of stars hurt Edgecombe's chances to put up numbers and earn his eventual rookie max extension?

Nights like Thursday show why those queries were silly.

"When I'm on the floor with those four other guys, it's kind of in a way where it's helpful for me," Edgecombe said, "because I'm in different positions and I'm in a year two — I'm seeing basketball from different standpoints. Some nights two of them might be out. Some nights one might be out. Some nights none might be out. And to be able to just balance the different lineups and just trying to stay consistent throughout it all.

"I just want to shout out my teammates for that. They just helped me a lot, especially 'Rese and Joel. They always just tell me keep going, keep going."

Anyone who watched Edgecombe as a rookie and heard him talk knew he'd excel with this star-studded group. Last season, Edgecombe's usage was unexpectedly high because the Sixers needed it to be. This season, they've needed Edgecombe to flash more of his overall game.

And 13 games in, that's exactly what he's done.

But when guys are out of the lineup, Edgecombe knows his team needs more.

"I think this is like the second time he's done this as well when we're down somebody that he just comes out a little bit more lively and assertive," Nick Nurse said. "And I thought … he made some really decisive moves in the open floor — not necessarily break situations, but the flow is still going, and he's punching through.

"And again, I thought he kicked out a few of those, and even on some of the half-court sets, he just attacked aggressively to the paint, and that's where some of those assists were coming from. So, I just thought he did come with an extra pop, and he's done that a couple times now this year."

When Edgecombe was coming out of Baylor, there were comparisons to old friend Jimmy Butler. This writer didn't see it at the time, but now it makes sense.

Some players simply impact winning. They affect the game in every facet. They stuff the stat sheet, but their imprint on the game goes far beyond it.

He's here, he's there, he's every-fucking-where. VJ, Edgecombe

"I'm just blessed to be in this position," Edgecombe said, "and at the end of the day, I just want to keep getting better. Some opportunities might not be there, but I think I'm getting better mentally, and that's the most important part of the game. Sometimes you got to be mentally strong."

#2866. Vikings down key pieces for Week 10 matchup with Packers

If the Minnesota Vikings want to repeat their Week 1 win over the Packers, they're going to have to do it shorthanded. They've already ruled out five players for Sunday's game, including tight end T.J. Hockenson and linebacker Blake Cashman.

Hockenson hasn't practiced yet this week with a combination of wrist and chest injuries. His absence will deprive the Vikings — and newly acquired quarterback Kirk Cousins — of one of their most productive receivers. Hockenson ranks first on the team with 35 catches and is second in yards with 328. Though he averages just 9.4 yards per catch, he'd have been a reliable and steady help for Cousins as he settles back into his familiar Minnesota environs.

Cashman, meanwhile, has been in the concussion protocol all week. He leads the Vikings with 76 total tackles and is also a key part of the Vikings' pressure scheme; his 4.5 sacks are the second most on the team, while his eight tackles for loss are tied for the team lead.

The Vikings also ruled out defensive lineman Tyrion Ingram-Dawkins, wide receiver Jauan Jennings, and quarterback Kyler Murray.

Though Murray's absence is basically immaterial at this point, Ingram-Dawkins and Jennings are noteworthy. Ingram-Dawkins was a significant part of the Vikings' defensive line rotation, playing just a hair under 58% of the Vikings' defensive snaps this year.

Jennings was more of a supporting player for the wide receiver-rich Vikings. Behind Justin Jefferson and Jordan Addison, Jennings had only logged six catches in six games. But Addison also finds himself questionable for Sunday, putting the Vikings in a bit of a wide receiver bind with Jennings already ruled out.

(And not for nothing, Jennings had five catches for 61 yards against the Packers in the 2023 playoffs as a member of the San Francisco 49ers. I just assume that any random role player who ever had a good game against the Packers has their number forever. It seems like it just works out that way.)

In any case, the Vikings won't be dealing from a full deck when they visit the Packers on Sunday. That's good news as the Packers try to continue their climb toward the top of the NFC North, especially since the Packers' injury report is anything but short as well.
