#2864. 10-year Treasury yield moves to 5.17% as traders weigh producer price report

The 10-year Treasury yield stayed over 5% on Friday after October's producer price index, a measure of wholesale prices, came in slightly less than expected.

The yield — the key benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — rose 4 basis points to 5.143%. It reached a high of 5.174% earlier in the day.

The 2-year Treasury note yield, typically more sensitive to short-term Federal Reserve interest rate decisions, was lower by less than 1 basis point at 4.859%. The longer-dated 30-year Treasury bond yield, sensitive to geopolitical risks, was more than 4 basis points higher at 5.472%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, and yields and prices move in opposite directions.

October's producer price index rose 0.3% versus September, less than the 0.4% expected by economists polled by Dow Jones. But on an annual basis, PPI rose 6.5% over the past 12 months, the biggest annual increase since November 2022.

There was "no silver lining" in the inflation report, said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at FWDBONDS, adding that core PPI — which excludes food, energy and trade services — increased 0.5% in October.

"It is economics 101 that producer prices lead consumer prices and right now it looks like consumers will be on the hook for a fresh burst of factory inflation," he said.

The moves come after 10-year Treasury yields jumped more than 13 basis points to more than 5% in Thursday's session, with the 2-year yield soaring more than 15 basis points.

Investors now see a Federal Reserve rate hike next month as a certainty. Traders are pricing in 100% odds of a rise at the Dec. 9 meeting, according to the CME Group's FedWatch tool. Odds of a quarter percentage point hike now stand at 89%, with the chance of a half point increase at around 14%.

Energy inflation remains a key part of the puzzle, with oil posting its second weekly decline as prospects of renewed strikes by the U.S. against Iran fade. U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures for December delivery were down 0.2% at $88.56 per barrel. Brent crude, the international oil benchmark, was higher by 0.02% at $95.84.

#2862. Women are taking pay cuts and 'white-knuckling it' to manage menopause and work: 'I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines'

Around 2020, Amy Terry noticed she didn't feel like her usual self.

The 50-year-old Michigander was working as a director of programming at a health association when she says she began experiencing symptoms like memory loss, low energy, mental processing difficulties — or, as she describes it, "waking up every day feeling like you were slogging through mud."

"I went from being this highly competent professional person to barely being able to get through phone calls," Terry says.

As the healthcare system strained under the weight of the Covid-19 pandemic, Terry, like many other people, struggled to get care. When she finally did, she found out it wasn't Covid-19. It was perimenopause.

Terry got through a few more years juggling her symptoms with her job, but by 2023, she couldn't hold out any longer. She quit to start independent consulting.

Women, and some trans and non-binary people, often experience career setbacks stemming from menopause that can hurt them financially and professionally. Four in 10 women who have experienced menopause or perimenopause say it has negatively affected their life or work, according to a recent survey of over 3,800 U.S. women from Gallup and Melinda French Gates' Pivotal, a group of organizations focused on social progress for women and young people.

Of them, roughly 28% felt limited in the effort they could give at work, 15% decided against pursuing a promotion or leadership role, 12% reduced their hours, and 10% took an extended leave from work or didn't return.

"This isn't just a health issue," says Jasmine Escalera, career expert at resume builder site LiveCareer. "It's a workplace issue."

A 2023 Mayo Clinic study estimated menopause symptoms drove $1.8 billion in lost work time per year in the U.S. alone.

Perimenopause, the transitional period before menopause, typically begins in one's 40s and can last several years, often bringing symptoms like hot flashes, sleep issues, irritability and lower energy, according to the Office on Women's Health. Menopause typically occurs in one's 50s and marks 12 months since a person's last period, but is often used colloquially to include the preceding perimenopause as well.

The timing of menopause can create a perfect storm. It frequently comes at a time in many women's careers when they're breaking into leadership or approaching their peak earning years, which is often between the ages of 35 and 44 for women, according to data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It also typically happens when many are caregiving for aging parents or raising children. This can make for an impossible choice for many women.

"Women have fought so hard for their place in the workforce," says Mimi Wied, who began perimenopause around a decade ago. "They don't want to lose it by looking like they can't handle it."

The career consequences for women "may increase the gaps we're trying to decrease," Escalera says, referring to disparities such as those in women's pay and senior-level representation relative to men. "That is a definite talent breakdown."

For Terry, becoming an independent consultant cost her employer-sponsored healthcare at the time she most needed it. It also drastically changed her income: She says she took around an 85% pay cut in the transition.

"These are supposed to be my highest-earning years," Terry says, "and I feel like I'm sitting on the sidelines."

Many women are unprepared for menopause because of social stigma around discussing it, poor health education and dismissal of women's health issues.

"I've been in the healthcare environment for 30 years; if I can't figure this out, I don't know how anyone can," says Terry.

Wied also says she didn't know what to expect. The answer quickly became apparent: The 48-year-old from Brenham, Texas, says she experienced everything from night sweats and sleeplessness to anxiety and rage.

"Sometimes I didn't feel in control of my own mouth and my body and my actions," she says.

Wied left her job as a museum docent for a secretarial role paying roughly $20,000 less when she worried her irritability would affect her capacity to help visitors, she says: "If I could have quit working, I would have."

When women do scale back at work because of menopause, they may struggle to regain that professional ground later. Terry's job switch wasn't just downshifting to a part-time role: "It's an entire change in the trajectory of your career," she says.

Factors like ageism in the job search for those of menopausal age, as well as the high cost of healthcare, particularly for common menopause treatments like hormone replacement therapy, may keep some women silently struggling through their symptoms at work.

Erin Levin is currently working through perimenopause. As a high school English teacher, the 41-year-old from San Jose, California, needs to stay on her A-game because "there is no down moment in teaching," she says.

But since starting perimenopause around two years ago, she's experienced brain fog, difficulty sleeping, and heightened anxiety and stress that make her work difficult sometimes, she says. She sometimes struggles with tasks that "were not an issue" for her a few years ago.

"Now I'm just like, God, why does it just feel so exhausting all the time?" Levin says.

Her parents help with childcare some evenings, and she tries not to schedule big tasks first thing in the morning, but it's been an adjustment. Scaling back at work isn't an option financially, she says, as a single mother sharing custody of her child.

"I still have to maintain the same appearance of having all the same skills and energy level as other people who aren't in my medical position," Levin says. "I just have to white-knuckle it."

Some women see hope in coming legislation and employer benefits offerings.

Last year, Rhode Island became the first state to enact workplace protections for women experiencing menopause. In September, Sens. Kirsten Gillibrand, D-N.Y., and Rick Scott, R-Fla., held the first congressional hearing devoted to menopause. Illinois and the city of Philadelphia have also adopted legislation taking effect in 2027 providing accommodations and protections for those in menopause.

In September, a nongovernmental organization, the International Organization for Standardization, published the first standard for developing workplace policies and practices supporting people through menstruation and menopause.

Many organizations have a ways to go in providing menopause-related benefits for their employees.

In a 2026 study from the Society for Women's Health Research and workplace menopause research organization The Fuchsia Tent, fielded among 1,100 people, around one-third of decision-makers said menopause support is currently in place or actively planned in their workplace. Fewer than 10% of employees said their organization offers menopause-specific support.

Respondents said some broader benefits, like flexible scheduling, remote options, and confidential telehealth or virtual care, would also be helpful for menopause.

Terry, Wied and Levin said they had to meet with multiple doctors before finding one who'd take their concerns seriously and who'd prescribe them hormone replacement therapy.

Levin and Wied each have a daughter. They hope better support and women's healthcare are available for their daughters when they begin menopause.

"I'm hoping that by the time my daughter goes through this, we'll have a lot more information, a lot more resources, because as it stands, it makes you super angry," Wied says.

"It's a cost on my energy, it's a cost on my stress levels, it's a cost on my bank account," Levin adds. "I don't want my daughter to have to face any of that."

As for Terry, she says she was part of a study examining potential mental health impacts of hormonal fluctuations in perimenopausal women; she hopes the research can help future generations of women. Meanwhile, her switch to independent consulting has offered her greater flexibility and a more manageable workload. But it's not perfect.

"I'm worried about retirement, I'm worried about having enough money, I'm worried about being able to continue healthcare," she says. "But I also feel like I have to take the time to feel like I'm on solid ground again."

#2861. Grading the Mavericks: Cooper Flagg is awesome, and he is only going to get better

The Mavericks went 3-1 over the last week and sit at 7-5 overall. They won three in a row at home against Utah (112-100), Brooklyn (130-93), and Indiana (125-122). Minnesota pummeled them to start off their road trip, 133-94. Cooper Flagg led the team in scoring with 25.3 points per game.

The Mavericks, once again, had a three-game win streak spoiled by an embarrassing loss. The Timberwolves are much better than the Kings, but the effort from Dallas was similar. Minnesota got any shot they wanted, exposed the Mavericks' size and lack of point-of-attack defenders, and effectively ended the game in the second quarter.

The three games prior, however, were great. The Mavericks took care of business against two bad teams and beat an Indiana team that always gives them problems. Morez Johnson Jr. has been awesome in his minutes; his early-season coup de grace was a 13-point, 17-rebound (10 offensive) performance against Utah, where he truly looked like the ninth pick for the first time. He said after the game that he was "back", implying that the injury that hindered him to start the year had dissipated.

Caleb Martin has been good in his role as defensive connector. P.J. Washington's jump shot looks so much better this season, likely because he is using his legs more. Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Irving's chemistry continues to develop as Irving gets healthier. Speaking of which, Irving played his first back-to-back of the season against Brooklyn and Indiana. Zaccharie Risacher has had his minutes go up and down, mostly correlated to how he is playing game to game. Naji Marshall is one piece that still has not felt right on this team, and his trade status will need to be monitored going forward.

The Mavericks have to wash the stink of the Timberwolves loss as they head to Cleveland and Boston on a Saturday/Sunday back-to-back, and then back home for Golden State on Wednesday.

Let's ignore Flagg's stinker against Minnesota for a second. In the three-game win streak prior, he averaged 29.7 points, 8.3 rebounds, 8.3 assists, shot seven-of-15 from three, and had nine stocks (steals plus blocks). This included a 43-point, 10-rebound game, which brings his 40-point game total as a teenager to five, the most all-time. He also sits only behind LeBron James in 25-point, 5-rebound, 5-assist games as a teenager (Flagg 17, James 22), with 13 games scheduled plus the NBA Cup before his 20th birthday,

He is awesome. He is a joy to watch, a fierce competitor, and improves (almost) every game. When his three-point shot is on, as it was over the last four games, he is truly unguardable at age 19. Of course, the preface to this section was ignoring his poor performance in Minnesota. He still has weird off-nights. But he is not even 20 years old, and those should phase out as he grows. One thing has been clear in 12 games so far: a team Cooper Flagg will not be able to lose very often.

Gafford has largely been good this year. He has been healthy, which is key, and he certainly is a lob threat on offense. But his weaknesses, particularly on defense, have been exposed recently. He cannot move in space effectively, which forces him to play drop coverage on pick-and-rolls. This means he drops back in the paint to guard the screener, which inherently leaves space for the ball handler to shoot or pass. Teams have been exploiting this, and it even got him benched against the Jazz.

His shot-blocking and big dunks are cool to watch, don't get me wrong. But Gafford has a bit of the "Boban Syndrome", where he is really good in short spurts and really bad when he plays extended periods. His minutes in the last four games are already down to just 18.3 a game, eclipsed by Morez Johnson Jr.'s 18.8. With Gafford, less is more. Unfortunately, this means they need their rookie undersized center, who is fresh off shin splints, will need to play more. Dallas may survive, but against bigger teams like the Timberwolves, they're going to struggle mightily.

Clayton Jr. has been one of the more underrated signings in the entirety of the NBA this season. His stats do not jump off the page (7.0 points, 3.0 rebounds, 3.5 assists per game), but when you watch him play, he looks like he belongs. This Dallas roster was in dire need of guard play, and Clayton Jr., last year's 18th overall pick, has given them great minutes at the one.

His assists come with just 1.6 turnovers per game, and he is shooting 37.1 percent from three. His hands on defense are active, which really stands out because he gets a lot of deflections. He is under control handling the ball, and already has the trust of the coaching staff to finish games. Normally, individual plus/minus is a flawed stat because there are four other guys on the court. However, Clayton Jr.'s +43 when he is on the floor this season backs up the eye test. He looks like an NBA point guard, and if he can keep this up, this would be Masai Ujiri's crown jewel early in his tenure.

#2859. ‘We will sue them’: Canada threatens Stellantis as Trump trade war reshapes Jeep production plans

Canada has threatened to sue auto giant Stellantis over the planned sale of an idled assembly plant in Brampton, Ontario, alleging breaches of a federal funding agreement after the carmaker announced plans to shift production to the U.S. amid President Donald Trump's trade war.

Speaking at a news conference on Thursday, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said the government had served Stellantis with a formal notice of default, giving the company 30 days to address its concerns or face legal action.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters in Toronto.

Joly said Stellantis had violated its contractual commitments to the Canadian government and the workers at Brampton by announcing an agreement to sell the plant to armored vehicle manufacturer Roshel earlier in the week. She added that the company was now "on the hook" for hundreds of millions of dollars over the move.

Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney has described the situation at the Brampton plant as "unacceptable" and a "betrayal" of its workers.

The standoff comes as the U.S. and Canada remain locked in a bitter trade dispute, with Trump repeatedly putting pressure on automakers to move vehicle production south of the border.

Stellantis, which owns auto brands including Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, had scrapped plans to build its Jeep Compass at the Brampton plant and shifted future production to a factory in Illinois amid U.S. auto tariffs and other market pressures.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said in a statement shared with CNBC that the company would return $24 million it received in government support for the Brampton Assembly's original retooling plan. This amount, however, falls significantly short of the Canadian government's own estimate of what the company owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said.

"As Canada's largest automotive employer, this decision also allows Stellantis to focus its resources on sustaining competitive manufacturing operations in Canada in the face of challenging market realities," he added.

Stellantis said Monday that it had agreed to sell the Brampton assembly plant to Roshel for an undisclosed amount.

It also acknowledged its obligations to employees at the facility and said it remains prepared to work with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union, to negotiate a settlement to assist union-represented employees.

Stellantis stock has fallen sharply since the start of the year, with its Milan-listed shares down more than 60% for 2026 so far.

— CNBC's Michael Wayland contributed to this report.

#2858. Whitaker sparks turnaround as Hamilton Heights upsets No. 7 Bluffton, wins 3A regional

ARCADIA - On Sept. 4, Hamilton Heights boasted a winless record after dropping its third straight to start the season. Two-plus months later, the unranked Huskies (10-3) haven't lost since and are headed to semistate in Class 3A after upsetting No. 7 Bluffton Friday night.

What happened in that first week of September that drastically altered the season's trajectory? Sophomore quarterback Lucas Whitaker happened.

After playing some in Week 3, Whitaker took over as the team's starting signal caller for the fourth game. Huskies' coach Caleb Small noticed a difference immediately.

"Our best week of practice was Week 4," Small said.

Whitaker, now 15, was only 14 when he made his first start against rival Tipton. He's shown poise well beyond his years since taking the job, on full display Friday as he made plays with both his arms and legs. He finished with three touchdowns in the win, two passing and one rushing.

The youngster deflected the hype to his coaches and teammates after the game.

"My coaches, they're advanced," Whitaker explained. "Our coach came from Ben Davis. He knows what he's doing. He's developed me really well."

Huskies senior tailback AJ Flanagan ran well for the hosts, often churning up difficult yards while dragging defenders with him. He produced two crucial rushing touchdowns in the second half as Bluffton kept matching Hamilton Heights blow for blow until the very end.

The Tigers scored with less than a minute to play to bring the score to 48-47 and opted to go for the win on a 2-point attempt. But poor execution led to the ensuing play breaking down as soon as the ball was snapped, allowing Hamilton Heights to hang on for the win after recovering an onside kick.

"We have a great running back and we have great wide receivers," Whitaker said. "Our o-line is very two dimensional. They can run block and pass block. As you saw, I think that was zero sacks today. If I have that much time to throw the ball, it makes everything way easier."

Bluffton senior running back Cooper Craig was a standout for the visitors, regularly gashing the Huskies for huge gains on the ground. He rushed for a game-high five scores, including touchdown runs of 35, 58 and 68 yards.

Tackling was an issue for the Hamilton Heights throughout, but its potent offense more than picked up the slack.

Bluffton (12-1), meanwhile, ends its season on a sour note after entering the contest undefeated.

For Whitaker and the Huskies though, a semistate date with second-ranked Andrean awaits. Indian Creek and Tri-West round out the other side of the Class 3A semistate bracket.

"We're in the final four for the first time in a long time," Small added. "Third time in program history. #2857. Josh Hart knows how sluggish Knicks can speed up their slow start

INDIANAPOLIS — Amid a struggling start for the Knicks wings, Josh Hart revealed he's dealt with injuries since pre-training camp and agreed Friday that the team's slow pace has negatively impacted himself, Mikal Bridges and OG Anunoby.

All three entered Friday's game against the Pacers shooting under 40 percent, while the Knicks were dead last in the league in pace at 95 possessions per game.

"There's less chances at the rim," Hart said. "I think it also affects Mikal and OG. … [Coach Mike Brown] says Mikal is the best runner in the league. So if you have the best runner in the league and you're not running, you're wasting one of his talents. If we play faster, we'll be able to do that. Sometimes OG has smaller guards defending him, so if we run he can be able to seal at the rim, be able to get easy points at the rim, which also can help our rim finishing."

Hart said the solution is to simply push the ball up quicker in possessions, even if that means Jalen Brunson isn't bringing up the ball. The Knicks were third in the NBA in frequency of field goals in the final four seconds of the shot clock, a stat indicative of a slowly evolving offense. They were also shooting an NBA-worst 57.2 percent in the paint, which is a symptom of few breakaways.

The Knicks were 23rd in the league in fastbreak points.

"We gotta get the ball out and sometimes that might have to be [Brunson] just running and not going to the ball after a make or a defensive rebound," Hart said. "Just trying to get a better and quicker flow so it's not so stagnant in the halfcourt."

Knicks guard Jalen Brunson #11 runs down court after scoring against the Bulls. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

The Knicks made up for some of their poor offense with offensive rebounding and second-chance points. Karl-Anthony Towns overall had a monster opening 10 games for New York.

Cavaliers guard Donovan Mitchell #45 drives to the basket as New York Knicks guard Josh Hart #3 gives chase. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

But Hart, Anunoby and especially Bridges weren't producing to their standard. Hart has been hindered by physical ailments. He planted his foot wrong in training camp and suffered an ankle injury, which caused him to miss consecutive games last week.

On Friday, Hart said he was sidelined for three weeks in September because "I tweaked my calf." It set back his conditioning and led to limitations in training camp, which Hart explained as a contributor to the clunky start.

"This year was a little different. Obviously, a shorter break in terms of the summer [because of the long playoff run] but also I wasn't physically able to really push my body towards the end of the summer to be prepared for the season," Hart said. "I had [the calf] injury in September, which kept me out for about three weeks, so I wasn't able to get in some of that conditioning and some of those legs under me, which is normally the time to do it.

"And then, the ankle injury in training camp. So, the ramp up was a little bit slower than I kinda wanted it to be or kinda liked it to be, so I think that's the biggest thing is just getting my legs under me."

Hart entered Friday shooting just 31.7 percent on 3-pointers — which is well below his average of 41.3 percent from last season.

"The shots I'm shooting, I like them. I think that was kinda evident with Cleveland. One of the things: All three of my 3s was just hella short. It was just, those legs, get those legs under me," Hart said. "Also, on top of that, missing a week in the season, it's cumulative of six, seven weeks of just not much activity basketball-wise. So, I think that's just what it is. So, as the season progresses and I'm out there running more, doing all those kinds of things, the performance will follow."

#2856. CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations 2026: How the 32 franchises stack up

For the first time, hockey team valuations have crossed the $4 billion threshold, with CNBC's NHL valuations led by the Toronto Maple Leafs, at $5.4 billion, the New York Rangers, at $4.7 billion, and the Montreal Canadiens, at $4.1 billion. The average NHL team is now worth $2.71 billion, 23% more than a year ago.

Don't be fooled by the lone NHL control sale to close over the past 12 months: the $1.8 billion sale of the Pittsburgh Penguins to the Hoffmann family. Although the transaction was approved by the league in June, the price was negotiated about 18 months ago.

Look instead at the latest minority stake transactions. In March the NHL approved the sale of a 12.5% stake in the Carolina Hurricanes by Tom Dundon in a transaction that valued the team at $2.66 billion. Scott Malkin and other shareholders are close to selling a 15% stake in the New York Islanders at a $3 billion valuation. Sports bankers tell CNBC these deals are representative of what control stakes in the two teams would sell for given the hot market for sports teams.

"Like the other major sports leagues there is a strong demand to own hockey teams because their media content values are high and there is a scarcity of teams," said Sal Galatioto, whose firm, Galatioto Sports Partners, advises clients on both the buy and sell sides of sports team transactions. "Capitalism and technology are creating more wealth and the NHL, like the NFL, NBA and MLB, is an industry with a very long product life cycle, a hedge against risk in AI."

The NHL's current national media rights deals with Walt Disney and Warner Bros. Discovery are worth a combined annual average of close to $650 million through the 2027-28 season, but given the league's strong television ratings last season, a big increase could be in the offing.

"The 2025-26 NHL regular season averaged 546,000 viewers across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports, up 25% from last year and the highest average in more than a decade," Sports Media Watch wrote. "The 25% increase is on the high end of a general rise in sports viewership that includes gains of 35% for the NBA, 19% for men's college basketball and 10% for the NFL."

Sports Media Watch also wrote that the 2026 NHL Stanley Cup Playoffs averaged 1.8 million viewers in the U.S. across ESPN/ABC and TNT Sports. That's up 68% from 2025, and it's the highest average on record for the playoffs on American television, going back to 1994, it said.

The NHL is pondering adding teams in cities such as Houston and Atlanta, and another factor fueling NHL valuations is the belief that the expansion fees paid by the new teams would surpass $2 billion. The Seattle Kraken, the NHL's most recent expansion team, paid a $650 million fee to join the league for the 2021-22 season.

According to CNBC's figures, revenue for the league's 32 teams averaged $257 million for the 2025-26 season, 6% more than the previous year, while EBITDA — earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization — averaged $48 million per team, a decline of 11% due to arena expenses. The league's profitability should rebound as money spent on hospitality, concessions and merchandise stores boosts revenue.

In April 2025 the NHL and Rogers Communications agreed to a 12-year national Canadian media rights deal worth $7.79 billion, based on exchange rates as of Nov. 24, that will begin with the 2026-27 season. That's more than twice the amount the league is getting from its existing 12-year deal with Rogers. This will help push up the league's hockey-related revenue — which includes media rights, sponsorships, premium seating, tickets, concessions and parking — to around $7.25 billion for the 2026-27 season, 7% more than last season.

CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations are current enterprise values — equity plus net debt — calculated using revenue multiples, and include the economics of the team's arena, including non-NHL revenue that accrues to the team's owner. The valuations are meant to represent what a control stake valuation would go for in an arm's-length transaction and do not include the value of the arena or the real estate it sits on.

Revenue figures are net of revenue sharing and the 35% of home playoff non-premium gate revenue that goes toward the league's revenue-sharing pool. Revenue multiples are based on historical transaction prices, as well as what investors, league executives and bankers say about the current market.

CNBC values exclude other businesses related to the teams with separate financial statements, such as the Edmonton Oilers' mixed-use real estate project. For teams that sell their own merchandise, such as the Oilers, CNBC deducts the cost of goods sold from merchandise sales. Likewise, CNBC excludes the market value of affiliates/equity method investments to ensure consistent enterprise value-to-revenue and enterprise value-to-EBITDA multiples. Debt figures are the latest available and include both team debt and stadium debt.

CNBC incorporates the value of a pending new arena in our valuations if construction has begun and financing has been secured. For example, the $2.25 billion valuation for the Calgary Flames incorporates their new arena, which is scheduled to open in time for the 2027-28 season. Likewise, CNBC valuations anticipate a substantial increase in the NHL's next U.S. national broadcasting deal, which would begin with the 2028-29 season.

Revenue and EBITDA figures are for the 2025-26 season and net of revenue sharing and player escrow. CNBC presents revenue and operating income figures on a cash basis, rather than an accrual basis. CNBC figures are different from the league's official hockey-related revenue because we include non-NHL arena revenue that goes to the teams. When the owner of the arena also owns both the NHL team and NBA team that play in the same building — as is the case for several teams, including Toronto's Maple Leafs and Raptors, who are both owned by Rogers Communications and play in Scotiabank Arena — we divide revenue from non-sports events held at the arena evenly between the two teams.

All valuation figures are in U.S. dollars. The exchange rate for revenue and EBITDA is based on a one-year average, from June 2025 to June 2026, of CA$1 = US$0.72, and the exchange rate for team values and debt, as of Sept. 21, is CA$1 = US$0.71.

Sources for CNBC's Official NHL Team Valuations include team owners, investors and executives, as well as sports bankers and league consultants; public documents such as stadium lease agreements, stadium authority budget audits and credit rating reports; and sponsorship and broadcasting industry executives. Certain figures were provided by league executives who spoke to CNBC on the condition of anonymity because agreements are private and they do not have permission to speak about them publicly. Figures that could not be confirmed with sources are CNBC estimates.

— CNBC's Jungwoo Ethan Kim contributed to this report.

#2855. U.S. seizes $1.2 billion in cryptocurrency stolen from Binance

U.S. authorities have seized around $1.2 billion in virtual assets stolen from cryptocurrency exchange Binance last month, the Department of Justice said Thursday, as it vowed action to tackle cyberscams originating from North Korea.

Over $6 billion was stolen from industry giant Binance on Oct. 29, which the DOJ attributed to actors linked to the North Korean military.

Collective efforts between the U.S. government and private sector have resulted in more than $2.7 billion of that amount being frozen.

The FBI blames the attack, which shook confidence in crypto market security, on members of the North Korean military it refers to as "TraderTraitor."

The DOJ said it was taking coordinated action against what it called the North Korean government's "schemes to fund its regime through the massive theft of cryptocurrency."

That includes "the court-authorized seizure and restraint of more than $1.2 billion worth of cryptocurrency recently stolen from Binance," it said.

DOJ units, law enforcement partners and virtual asset service providers have been working to intercept the stolen assets.

But efforts to track and freeze the assets are ongoing, as TraderTraitor actors continue to launder them through various cryptocurrency channels.

Authorities have also seized cloud-based infrastructure used in a massive identity impersonation scam known as "FaceStealer," which has seen more than $400 million worth of cryptocurrency stolen over 18 months, the DOJ said.

The scheme sees hackers set up fake video meetings using stolen accounts and recordings of people known to the target by using AI-generated deepfakes. The hackers then say a technical problem requires a fix, install malware on a computer and take over the victim's communications, steal their cryptocurrency and target their contacts — sometimes months or more than a year after the initial compromise.

Individuals working in the cryptocurrency and web3 industries are prime targets.

Assistant Director in Charge Patrick Grandy, of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office, called FaceStealer "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user."

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships. If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," Grandy added.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor said in a statement.

"America will fight back against that corrupt regime's effort to profit through cybercrime. The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

#2854. LaMelo Ball sure looks like the difference: Timberwolves and Hornets trending in opposite directions after trade

We will stipulate that, when it comes to success and struggle in the NBA, the answer is never just one thing. There's plenty that goes into teams' fortunes rising and falling, and all sorts of stuff can factor into the difference between living in the penthouse and taking up residence in the outhouse.

With that caveat in place: While the Minnesota Timberwolves were doing this in Minneapolis on Thursday, en route to dismantling the Dallas Mavericks, 133-94 …

… and the Charlotte Hornets were doing this in Phoenix on the same night, en route to a desultory 101-96 defeat …

… it was hard not to note the Just One Thing that seems to be contributing to the difference between Minnesota and Charlotte. Or, rather, the Guy Wearing No. 1.

After walloping a pretty good Mavs team, the Wolves are 9-3, in third place in the Western Conference, with the NBA's best net rating. They've won five in a row, and six out of eight since losing superstar No. 1 option Anthony Edwards to knee surgery. They've outscored their opponents by a total of 161 points this season; according to Justin Kubatko of Statitudes, that's the largest point differential through 12 games in Wolves franchise history — by 50 points.

After falling in Phoenix, mustering just 96 points against the league's fourth-worst defense, the Hornets are now 4-8, tied for 10th in the East, with the NBA's No. 26 net rating. They've lost seven of eight, have logged more turnovers than assists four times in 12 games, and have already failed to crack 100 points six times — as many times as Charlotte finished south of the century mark all of last season.

Again: It's not just that LaMelo Ball used to play for the Hornets, and now he plays for the Wolves. The operating theory for how Charlotte would generate offense in the absence of its one-time linchpin rested largely on continued growth and elevated production from Brandon Miller, Kon Knueppel and the re-signed Coby White, with a dash of second-unit contribution from veteran Dennis Schröder. The Hornets have gotten a grand total of 97 minutes thus far from White, who's on a minutes restriction as he works his way back from a left calf strain, and Knueppel, who'll miss the next several weeks with a balky left hamstring. Schröder's also on the shelf with a hamstring strain; Miller, the team's leading scorer, has missed the last two games with a groin injury.

Any team would struggle to score without the bulk of its perimeter firepower, ball-handling and shot creation. It has to be painful, though, to be mired in such a horrific offensive morass mere months after consciously choosing to bid farewell to Ball, the player whom both the eye test and the advanced numbers suggested served as the rising tide that lifted Charlotte's offense last season.

And it must be particularly galling to be suffering through the doldrums while watching LaMelo have exactly the same kind of effusive, elevating effect in the Twin Cities.

Ball had nine of the Wolves' 33 assists in the blowout win over the Mavericks on Thursday — Minnesota's fourth 30-assist game of the season, more than anybody but the Hawks, Warriors, Nuggets and Spurs. Those helpers led to 19 Minnesota points, in addition to the 22 that Ball scored himself; for the season, he's directly creating 56.9 points of offense per 100 possessions for the Wolves, according to Databallr, which is 20th in the NBA — and nearly five points-per-100 more than White, who leads this season's Hornets.

The Wolves generate a higher share of their shots at the rim when LaMelo's on the floor than when he's off it. Their 3-point shooting goes through the roof in his minutes, and nearly every member of the team sees a bump in shot quality when playing alongside him.

The Wolves are more efficient in the half-court when Ball's at the controls, and they get out in transition way more often with him out there. Minnesota's team-wide effective field-goal percentage spikes by 6.5% with LaMelo on the floor — the largest on/off split on the team, and one of the largest among all NBA players to log at least 200 minutes this season, according to Cleaning the Glass.

The numbers tell a compelling story. So, too, does the joy that seems to leap off the screen from so many of the Wolves as they run with LaMelo — orbiting, emulating and ultimately benefiting from the irrepressible style and flow with which he plays.

"I think when you have a guy like Melo, he makes it fun for everybody," Timberwolves head coach Chris Finch said after Thursday's win, according to Sarah Todd of the Minnesota Star Tribune. "And the guys can trust in the next pass, next play."

That trust is tougher to come by these days in Charlotte, where the Hornets enter Friday's action ranked dead last in the NBA in points scored per possession, field-goal percentage, free-throw attempts, assists and turnovers, struggling to consistently string together quality possessions, even against lesser defensive outfits.

"How do we stop the bleeding?" White told reporters after a recent loss to the Detroit Pistons. "There's gonna be turnovers. There's gonna be miscommunications on both sides of the ball. How do we not let it continue to derail us from what we're trying to do?"

The loss of a superstar of Edwards' magnitude would've derailed plenty of teams. The Wolves, though, have continued to thrive, buoyed in part by the NBA's No. 1 defense — a unit on which Ball has somewhat surprisingly emerged as a physical and positive contributor at the point of attack. All told, Minnesota has outscored opponents by a whopping 15.5 points per 100 possessions when Ball has played without Edwards — a loftier net rating than even their regular-season leading mark, and more than good enough to stay afloat in the brutally competitive West while Ant continues to rehab his ailing knee.

There are plenty of other factors at play. The Hornets have underperformed their expected shot quality by more than any other team in the NBA thus far, according to Synergy Sports Technology's tracking; the combination of some regression to the mean and the hopefully healthy return of some key pieces should help bolster their offense. And there's plenty of credit to go around in Minnesota, from Rudy Gobert's perpetually stalwart work on the back line to Finch getting career-best play from the likes of Jonathan Kuminga, Ayo Dosunmu and Bones Hyland to a collective closing kick that has the Wolves ranked second in the NBA in fourth-quarter net rating.

"We've definitely [elevated] our play in the fourth quarters on both sides of the ball," Finch said. "Getting key stops, rebounding really well, executing, finding really good shots. And LaMelo's been at the heart of a lot of that on offense."

LaMelo's presence has been at the heart of a lot of what's gone well for the Wolves, and his absence, it seems, has been at the heart of a lot of what's haunting the Hornets. It's never just one thing. Sometimes, though, one thing can make one hell of a big difference.

#2853. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2852. Jensen Huang scandal casts doubt on Nvidia’s AI dominance — how we got here

Nvidia is facing one the biggest crises in its 33 year history, with founder Jensen Huang resigning as CEO Thursday after multiple reported affairs, including one with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy. Now CFO Colette Kress has taken the helm as Nvidia's interim CEO.

The implications of Huang's affair with Alice Huang run far deeper than infidelity. That's because her ties to Nvidia customer Megaspeed put her in the middle of a sprawling effort to accumulate AI chips across Southeast Asia. And Megaspeed is under investigation for possibly diverting restricted Nvidia chips into the hands of Chinese companies.

The relationship between Jensen Huang and Alice Huang dates back to 2023, according to The New York Times. That was a "turbulent" period for Nvidia, Jensen Huang told CNBC during an interview at the time. Nvidia was in the midst of redesigning some chips to comply with the first in a series of strict export controls imposed by the U.S. government that restricted sales to China.

"The regulation is going to be just fine. We're going to be able to work through it," Huang told CNBC from Nvidia's headquarters in February 2023. The interview was for a documentary about how Huang created Nvidia and built it into the backbone of the AI boom.

That same day, CNBC also interviewed Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, with whom Jensen Huang reportedly had an affair.

It's not the first time Nvidia has faced an uncertain future. The company nearly went bankrupt in the early years after it was founded in 1993.

"Every company makes mistakes and I make a lot of them," Huang said in the 2023 interview. "And some of them put the company in peril."

In the early months of the AI boom, after OpenAI launched ChatGPT in November 2022, Nvidia was on the precipice of historic growth. Jensen Huang told CNBC he'd like to keep running the company for "three or four decades," as long as "I feel that I'm making a real contribution to the company."

Less than four years later, Jensen Huang is out, and we're taking a look back at our interviews with the Nvidia co-founder and other company executives as the chipmaker enters this next critical chapter. Watch the video for the full story.

#2851. Trump says he wants to ‘get Cuba done’ before leaving office, would consider military force

U.S. President Donald Trump has said he wants to "get Cuba done" before leaving office in 2029, adding that he would "certainly consider" military intervention against the communist-run Caribbean island.

In an interview with conservative radio host Hugh Hewitt on Thursday, Trump said his administration was monitoring the situation in Havana "very closely" and described Cuba as a "failed" state.

"I want to get Cuba done before I leave," Trump said. His comments marked the first time he has put a timeline on possible military intervention to topple Cuban authorities, according to Politico.

Trump said Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose parents are from Cuba, "would love to do it" and said he would "certainly consider" a short military operation.

It comes as the U.S. continues to enforce a comprehensive economic embargo against Cuba, a campaign which has contributed to a deepening energy and economic crisis on the island. Cuba has suffered repeated nationwide blackouts in recent months, as well as fuel and food shortages.

The Cuban Embassy in Washington, D.C., estimated on Thursday that the cost of the U.S. blockade to Cuba between March 2025 and February 2026 amounted to $8 billion.

Earlier this month, the Treasury Department imposed further sanctions on Cuban oil tankers and shipping companies and threatened to target any international entity or person doing business within its marine or maritime industries as part of a push "to end the Cuban regime's malign activities, both in Cuba and across our hemisphere."

In response, Cuba's Embassy in Washington, D.C., said these steps "deepen an energy siege that already leaves hospitals without reliable power and families facing blackouts. The blockade is not targeted at a government. It is collective punishment that hits ordinary people every day."

Trump has previously talked up the prospect of a "friendly takeover" of Havana and said the White House could turn its sights on Cuba after the Iran war. The U.S. president has also said he could do anything he wanted with the country, adding that he thinks he will have the "honor" of "taking Cuba."

In May, Cuban Energy Minister Vicente de la O Levy said the country of roughly 10 million had run out of fuel oil and diesel, describing the country's predicament as "extremely tense."

Analysts have typically been skeptical about the prospect of a U.S. military campaign against Cuba, suggesting that further economic pressure against the island's authorities appears more likely.

#2849. Apple, Alphabet and Uber survived major founder transitions. Nvidia will be a harder test

Jensen Huang is not the first CEO to leave a company he helped build.

But experts told CNBC that his sudden resignation from Nvidia at a critical juncture is unique and leaves his eventual successor with challenges in leading the company that's powering the artificial intelligence boom.

In 33 years, Huang, who resigned on Thursday amid reports of extramarital affairs, including with a woman U.S. intelligence believes is a spy for China, made the company he co-founded the world's most valuable.

Former Goldman Sachs CEO Lloyd Blankfein wrote on X that he was "indispensable" but "the graveyards are filled with indispensable men," referencing a quote attributed to French leader Charles de Gaulle.

While the board looks for a new CEO and CFO Colette Kress does the job temporarily; analysts say Huang's charisma and bullish drive will be hard to replicate.

"I'm not sure we've seen a situation quite like this, where the founder and CEO of one of the world's largest companies faces such a sudden departure amid scandal," Michael Withers, professor of management at the University of Notre Dame's Mendoza College of Business, told CNBC.

Whoever follows will "face challenges that go well beyond those of a typical CEO replacing a founder," he added.

Apple's image was inseparable from Steve Jobs when he resigned in 2011. But unlike Nvidia, the company had had other CEOs — and an internal successor.

Tim Cook invested in Apple's chip strategy and services business and became a key corporate diplomat in dealings with the U.S. and Chinese governments. He was known as an operator who controlled Apple's supply chain.

Cook had "considerable preparation" for becoming CEO, Withers said. Apple's market cap went from about $350 billion to $4 trillion in his 15-year tenure.

Larry Page and Sergey Brin left their executive roles at Alphabet in 2019, 21 years after founding Google.

Page and Brin's departures were expected. Page said at the time that it was the "natural time" to simplify the company's management structure. Alphabet shares are up more than 300% since then under Sunder Pichai.

Travis Kalanick left Uber in 2017 after mounting pressure over a series of scandals and governance concerns.

But his departure "unfolded over several months, and while it certainly attracted attention, it pales in comparison to what Nvidia is currently facing," Withers added.

Former Expedia CEO Dara Khosrowshahi repaired the organization and found a path to profitability.

Withers said successor CEOs often struggle "under normal circumstances" to juggle maintaining what made a company successful while establishing their own leadership.

"It becomes considerably more difficult when the transition follows such a high-profile scandal involving the company's founder," he added.

Huang had an extraordinary 60 direct reports, as part of a flat structure that favored fast communication over layered hierarchies.

Withers told CNBC Nvidia did not appear to have a succession plan for Huang, who is also often described as inextricably linked to the company.

"We're talking about a company that was, until recently, the largest in the world by market capitalization. That's a pretty remarkable situation," he added.

As well as having to "step out of his shadow," the new CEO will have to deal with the "scandal" arising from Huang's conduct and "the extent to which it sticks with Nvidia," Withers said.

Huang's successor will also have to tackle the longer-term business strategy, while picking up the relationships he managed.

Huang cultivated a close relationship with U.S. President Donald Trump and accompanied him to China in May. Huang consistently pushed for the U.S. to relax export restrictions that prevent Nvidia from selling its chips to the world's second-largest economy.

He also managed close relationships with the bosses of the company's key suppliers, from SK Hynix to Samsung.

Storm Uru, co-head of the Global Innovation team at Nvidia investor Liontrust Asset Management, said two key priorities were changing company culture and managing the supply chain.

The supply chain was "where Jensen was able to provide a vision for the future and able to bring people to the table and to expand capacity to enable the build-out of this technology," Uru told CNBC, adding it mattered especially "when supply chains are already so tight."

Nvidia continues to face rising competition from rivals such as AMD and hyperscalers, which are ramping up efforts to develop their own chips.

Investors noted that the new CEO will have a roadmap of products for the next few years, unaffected by Huang's removal.

"We have to look beyond Jensen ... there are thousands upon thousands of engineers," Joanne Feeney, partner and portfolio manager, Advisors Capital Management, told CNBC on Thursday, as pressure was mounting on Huang to go.

#2848. Why the data center slowdown concern is overblown. Two stocks to buy amid the fear

The demise of data centers has been greatly exaggerated. So says every utility CEO and energy-focused investor we spoke with at the Edison Electric Institute's annual conference in Arizona. Here's why...

There is a lot of misleading, misdirected, or straight-up incorrect information floating around about data centers and the AI buildout. I'm not going to dive into the old water-use story — you can do your own research on that — but rather focus this week on what the insiders know: Some newly elected politicians either don't have the power to stop the buildouts or, if they do, the buildout will likely just continue somewhere else.

Ohio and Georgia are important states to watch with their new incoming political class, but for the sheer importance in size and scope, I'll focus on the energy and data center capital: Texas.

Incoming Governor Gina Hinojosa and incoming Senator James Talarico both campaigned on promises to go after what they believe to be affordability issues around data centers. I say "what they believe to be" because it's unclear how much data centers may raise your electricity bill, if at all.

First and foremost, data centers are largely a state issue, meaning officials elected to federal office have little control over outcomes, at least for now. In Texas, any cancellations or moratoriums on new builds will come from the statehouse in Austin. That's where current Governor Greg Abbott has already, and rather quietly, put his own "pause" on data center permitting. That delay has been underway for a few weeks. Also, in Texas, the Governor has less direct power than in some other states. The power the governor does have largely derives from the authority to appoint those in charge of the all-important permitting process.

In the Lone Star State, that power lies with two agencies: the Public Utility Commission of Texas (PUCT) and the even lesser-known Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ). The PUCT regulates Texas' electricity, telecom, and water industries. It helps oversee growth of anything electricity-grid-related. So if a data center wants to connect to the public utility grid, it needs the commission's approval. Its chair — a pro-growth chap named Thomas Gleeson —is also on the board of Texas' electric grid powerhouse ERCOT and will serve his term with the utility commission through September 2029. Yes, three years from now.

That agency gets involved when a data center or power provider wants to connect to the electricity grid. But what about facilities that are off the grid and have their own dedicated power sources? Texas has less control over what happens then. That said, it does have to authorize certain environmental permits, and that responsibility largely lies with the TCEQ. The TCEQ was "BBI" (my own little term for "boring, but important") until data centers came along. It's not exactly the Alamo, but this obscure group — run by a regulator named Brooke Paup — is largely the "last stand" for permitting on those new builds. Paup was also appointed by Gov. Abbott and serves through next year. In other words, unless she and Gleeson step down or there is some way to oust them, the regulatory environment in Texas may not change much.

The bottom line in this brief foray into the Texas regulatory swamp is to highlight that there are things for investors to consider around the data center/energy world that may not be at the top of the headlines.

Important Note → Be mindful that I am an East Coast-based financial broadcaster and journalist, not an expert on Texas regulation or how the state operates. There may very well be things I don't know, things my sources aren't aware of, or other ways in which the new Governor and state House can assert power. For one, she recently said she may try to freeze electricity rates. Hint: that would be a terrible idea unless you want to damage the growth of badly needed new power sources like wind and solar.

Disclaimer is done. Let's assume I am wrong on Texas and there is a larger push to slow or stop data center growth in the state. Conversations with some pretty smart people lead me to believe that if those facilities aren't built in that state, they will be built in another: Louisiana, Oklahoma, or another state. Sources tell me that Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry and his team are already working the phones, telling companies that his state would love to welcome new business. Same for Oklahoma. Both are gas-rich, have plenty of power, and are only too happy to grab any data center dollars.

Let's acknowledge that not all data centers are going to be built… and never were. There are way too many plans to make them all happen. Some DCs were shelved before the election. Some others may soon be canceled, either for reasons of public pushback or otherwise. It's also not clear if we even need all the planned compute. There will be a time when companies realize they have enough — or at least enough for now — and turn off the spending taps.

Goldman Sachs lays it out in a new note late this week:

"The midterm election results pose only modest risks to AI investment, for now. We expect newly elected Democratic governors in states that account for over a third of planned US data center capacity to impose moratoria on new data centers. However, these will likely only impact projects not yet permitted, leaving the vast majority of planned 2027 construction unaffected. We therefore expect data center construction spending to remain strong next year, though moratoria that are stricter or longer-lasting than we expect pose downside risks."

The firm adds that it still sees spending on AI-related hardware coming in around $1.4 trillion by the end of next year. In other words, the spending boom is going to keep going, at least for now.

Another key point for all you energy- or utility-focused investors out there: While data centers are one huge driver of electricity demand, they aren't the only one. Electric cars, reshoring manufacturing, and the "electrification of everything" will also grow the need for more gigawatts. This is why we asked every utility CEO we spoke with at the EEI conference if they saw any slowdown in their internal electricity growth estimates. All gave the same answer: no. Not one hinted at a downturn. Not Exelon's Calvin Butler. Not Entergy's Drew Marsh. And not Drew Maloney, head of EEI. Of course, power demand is about more than just data centers, but it's still important for investors to hear it from the top.

Side note: Many of you think I'm wrong about my "hot take" on data centers and the election. If you missed last week's Power Insider, this is how you responded to my X poll on the election and energy.

So… why am I writing this week about AI and data centers? Because, as I've said before and will keep saying, AI and data centers are just energy stories in disguise. If they grow, energy demand grows. Demand for solar, wind, and natural gas grows. The economy grows. Because, after all, energy is the economy.

Oh, and in the past week, I've flown from Newark to Rome to Chicago to Phoenix and back to Newark. My brain may be a little mushy, so bear with me.

So now that my big take on data centers and regulation is over, let's get granular. What energy stocks do you buy right now?

Let's get the "1-2-3" on six stocks: one in solar, two in power, and three in oil and gas.

One: William Blair is upgrading First Solar (FSLR) to Outperform. The election result is part of the story. Analyst Jed Dorsheimer and his team believe the new Congress and certain state governorships will benefit local support for utility-scale solar, and that First Solar is best-positioned, though he does caution investors that it may take "some time to show up in the bookings." He adds that Texas is the largest builder of solar, so the shift in state-level leadership is a big deal. Fair warning, however: upgrades do not mean the stock will go higher. Deutsche Bank upgraded FSLR back in early July — an upgrade I wrote about — and the stock has fallen 22% since.

Two: Staying with the macro power theme, Barclays' Nicholas Campanella is particularly optimistic on two utility names. He tells Power Insider that he "really likes NiSource (NI) and Xcel Energy (XEL) right now." It has to do in part with what I just wrote about above. Campanella says the political outcomes in states such as Texas and Georgia may drive investors into utilities in other areas, particularly those with "more stable and established data center policies in place." That would fit with Indiana-based NiSource and Minnesota-based Xcel. While Minnesota may have a Democratic tilt, the company operates in eight different states. It is also working with Alphabet (GOOGL) on a massive new data center, and CEO Bob Frenzel told us that he is confident they can successfully navigate it all.

Three: Now to the "3"… three picks on what powers some of the power: oil and natural gas.

In an interview on CNBC, Bernstein analyst Bob Brackett says he likes ExxonMobil (XOM), calling it a great "barbell" play between natural gas and refined products. Brackett — a former Exxon employee — also believes it's a relatively "safe" stock to own right now in the volatile and headline-driven world of energy. Brackett has an Outperform rating and $186 target on the stock, giving it about another 10% upside from here.

Brackett also likes the lesser-known Expand Energy (EXE). If you're not familiar with Expand Energy, don't worry; it's still relatively new. The company is the combination of Chesapeake Energy and Southwestern Energy. It's a natural gas behemoth, and Brackett says the gas story is a "great long-term story" that's trading below a "midcycle price," and Expand fits right into it. Brackett also notes that Expand has a new CEO. He has a $139 target on the stock, making him one of the more bullish analysts on the Street. Expand has a median price target of $123.54.

Meanwhile, the chart looks tasty on another energy name: Cheniere Energy (LNG). Piper Sandler technical guru Craig Johnson likes the stock, and while he isn't a fundamental energy analyst, he was one of the few to upgrade the entire sector back in February when the Iran war began. Cheniere — along with Venture Global (VG) — is a pure play on why I was just in Europe: the worsening European energy crisis. Cheniere exports massive quantities of U.S. natural gas to customers in Europe, Asia, and other parts of the world. Johnson likes Cheniere in part because it recently broke out of a "very big base," and he wants to buy stocks that are on the upswing.

Cheniere is a well-run, rather quiet company with a fantastic CEO in longtime energy executive Jack Fusco. Jack has forgotten more about running an energy business than most will know, and the company continues to expand production. As I've pointed out for over four years, without American natural gas, Europe would be in dire straits and cutting demand even more than it already is. It's not hyperbole to say that American natural gas is — literally — keeping the lights and the heat on across Germany, Italy, and other countries.

Remember this incredible stat: just one tanker of LNG can produce a year's worth of power for over 100,000 average-sized European homes. Next year, Europe is still planning to stop buying LNG from Russia. That means demand for American natural gas is only going one way: up.

This week's Inside Line is with Amrita Sen, founder and director of market intelligence at Energy Aspects. Amrita is one of the smartest and most well-connected energy experts in the world, and she took a few minutes from her constant travel to answer a couple of questions.

This week's Take A Look is a little outside the norm, but an important interview nonetheless. It's with the U.S. Ambassador to Italy, business magnate, and former star of CNBC's Billion Dollar Buyer, Tilman Fertitta. In a wide-ranging interview from the ambassador's residence in Rome, we talked about that day's stock market crash, business, the consumer, and, of course, energy. Full disclosure: Tilman and I have been friends for over 15 years.

This Washington Post article was published a few days after the election. The underlying research from Harvard University and the University of Pennsylvania that it was based on is here and is worth a read. The study came out on October 9 (and yes, I missed it as well).

Not blaming data centers for higher energy costs is definitely random, but interesting!

#2847. Draymond Green shoves Victor Wembanyama, earns Flagrant 1 in Warriors-Spurs game

Draymond Green shoves Victor Wembanyama, earns Flagrant 1 in Warriors-Spurs game originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

It was only a matter of time before Draymond Green earned his first flagrant foul of the 2026-27 NBA season, and the Warriors veteran crossed it off during Golden State's NBA Cup game against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday night at Frost Bank Center.

Green sent Victor Wembanyama to the floor with 6:47 left in the second quarter as the Warriors trailed 45-22, making contract with the Spurs star's back that officials deemed included a "wind up" by Green. The hit earned Green a Flagrant 1 foul — his first of the season and 21st of his NBA career, including the playoffs.

Green is known for his physicality, and could have been trying to light a spark under the Steph Curry-less Warriors as they struggled early against the Spurs. But the action certainly was unwarranted, and he paid a price for it.

The 36-year-old was ejected following an on-court altercation against the Phoenix Suns on Nov. 2 that earned him back-to-back technical fouls, and many on the Warriors credited the fiery exchange for inspiring their eventual win.

Already short-handed and without veteran leaders like the injured Curry and Jimmy Butler, Golden State needs Green to stay out of trouble and remain on the floor as often as he can.

#2846. Chicago Fed's Goolsbee says central bank 'has definitely got work to do' on inflation

Chicago Federal Reserve President Austan Goolsbee expressed concern about the latest inflation data, telling CNBC on Friday that the central bank likely will need to take action.

"I believe that the the central bank has definitely got work to do to continue to reestablish its credibility," the policymaker said during an interview on "Squawk Box."

His remarks came shortly after the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported that the producer price index, considered a proxy for wholesale prices, posted a 0.3% increase in October, which was slightly less than expected, but also indicated a 6.5% annual gain, the highest since November 2022.

With data in hand earlier in the week showing that the consumer price index rose a bit more than expected during the month, Goolsbee said the Fed is slipping further away from its 2% inflation target.

"We've been above 2% for almost six years, and while it sometimes we were making progress, lately we're not making any progress, and if anything, it's going the wrong way," he said.

"Inflation is rising. It's proved far more persistent than we predicted at the beginning from the supply shocks, and now we're adding this old-fashioned demand overheating coming from the AI data center buildout, that looks like it's starting to spread out of its lane," Goolsbee added.

Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point increase when the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee meets next on Dec. 8-9. There also is a better than 10% probability of a half-point hike, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge of futures markets. Goolsbee is not currently an FOMC voter but will be in 2027.

Earlier in the week, Fed Governor Christopher Waller indicated he would entertain a 50 basis point move depending on this week's inflation data.

Goolsbee did not speculate directly on whether the Fed might need to be more aggressive.

"It's it's all going to be about this ... question: What is happening to inflation, and are we on path back to 2%?" he said. "My feeling is, right now we're not."

#2845. Hackers stole $6.3 billion from Binance—2 habits that take 'a few seconds' can help you avoid similar traps

The biggest heist in cryptocurrency history began with small mistakes many internet users make. A hacking group tied to North Korea recently stole $6.3 billion from the crypto exchange Binance. The operation was sophisticated, involving fake job interviews and malicious code.

But it was also mundane. Although artificial intelligence played a role in the hack, this was not a cyberattack of the future, propelled by swarms of AI agents. Rather, it relied on humans who made some basic errors, like clicking a link because it appeared first on a search results page or trusting strangers at a fake company without taking a few seconds to look them up.

We work at the Digital Inquiry Group, a nonprofit that researches how people evaluate online information and creates free, evidence-based curriculum that helps people decide what to trust.

In over a decade of research, we've found that people frequently engage in online behavior that makes them more likely to get duped. Two simple strategies can help you better navigate today's information landscape, from traditional phishing to misleading social posts to AI fakes. Here's how.

The Binance heist began when one of the company's engineers downloaded compromised software. That engineer, according to the Reuters investigation of the hack, clicked on a sponsored search result that appeared higher on the results page and directed them to the malicious code.

Our research at the Digital Inquiry Group suggests internet users frequently behave as if first is best. They click on the first thing they see. They incorrectly associate a higher placement with vetted content, ceding judgment to tech company algorithms. Students have gone so far as to tell us "Google is almost never wrong" or "AI is always right."

Professional fact-checkers commonly practice click restraint. They understand that whether you're searching on the internet or querying a chatbot, first isn't always best. If you're researching an organization, the first link will often be to its own site, which is hardly likely to disclose anything that's wrong with it.

So the next time you search for something online, be wary of links to sponsored content, the organization you're investigating, or to open platforms where anyone can post and information isn't verified, such as Quora or Reddit. Read the brief snippets beneath each link, and make a more informed choice about what to open.

Always ask yourself: Who is behind this information? What makes them credible? Look for people and organizations that have direct expertise on the topic at hand, or have processes to ensure the accuracy of information, such as news outlets that employ editors and fact-checkers.

Binance says it caught that first malicious download, replaced the device and changed passwords. But later, another engineer downloaded compromised code as part of what they thought was a job interview, which included a virtual meeting with the supposed CEO of a fake company.

It's tempting to think that the engineer should have seen something was off. But the internet is a precarious place because bad actors know how to make content feel legitimate. Binance says its employee was snared by a hacker posing as an HR rep who "shared a social media account with him, including personal photos and videos posted on the platform, in order to build trust."

When they encounter a piece of unfamiliar content, professional fact-checkers don't just read it closely — they read laterally. They open new browser tabs to see what other, credible sources have to say. Fact-checkers know that bad actors make themselves look good on their home turf, but that it's a lot harder to fake a good reputation across the internet.

The next time you encounter something unfamiliar online, look for authoritative sources that can shed light on the person or organization. You might find a news article that confirms the claim you're looking into, or a report that identifies a supposedly neutral source as biased.

In some cases, you may find nothing at all, which is a signal in itself: if you look up a company that reached out to you and find no external confirmation of its existence, that should be an immediate red flag.

Had that first Binance engineer engaged in click restraint and verified the source of the software, they might have avoided the malicious download. Had that second employee taken time to seek external verification about the supposed HR rep, rather than trusting a polished social media profile, they might have realized that something didn't add up.

The internet and AI have made it harder to know whether what we see is authentic. They've also made it easier than ever before to investigate who or what we encounter online. It's always worth pausing to check. A few seconds up front can save us all a lot of time, grief and money later on.

Nadav Ziv is a researcher at the Digital Inquiry Group. Joel Breakstone is co-founder and executive director of the Digital Inquiry Group.
